    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. I do hope I'm not bothering you with something trivial. \n\nWell... I suppose I came in because, oh, it's a bit embarrassing to say out loud. It's just a silly little habit, really, but I seem to be spending far too much of my day... well, opening the same folders and drawers. And my little labeled boxes. I just like to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them. \n\nEven when I've, you know, just checked them a few minutes before, I find myself going back. I just want to be absolutely certain they're safe. Is that... does that make sense to you? I hope it doesn't sound too terribly foolish.\n\nDoctor: Does this habit of checking your folders and drawers cause you a lot of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say it causes a lot of stress. I certainly don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill! \n\nWell... perhaps just a tiny bit of, oh, fretfulness, I suppose? Just until I look. But once I check on things, I feel perfectly fine. It’s really just a matter of wanting to be thorough and... and keeping my affairs in order. I wouldn't call it terrible anxiety at all. \n\nI do hope I'm explaining this properly to you. It’s quite normal to want to be careful with one's things at my age, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your folders and drawers, or could you stop doing it if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose I could stop if I really put my mind to it. I mean, I'm not... I'm not out of control or anything like that. I certainly don't want you to think I've lost my marbles! \n\nIt's just that... well, if I try to stop, or if I tell myself, \"You don't need to look in that box again,\" I just get this nagging little feeling. It’s silly, really. I just think about those important papers—my birth certificate, the deed, the little trinkets from my late husband—and I think, \"What if they've been misplaced?\" \n\nSo, I suppose I do feel a... a strong urge to just go and peek. Just to be absolutely sure they are right where I left them. And I do end up opening the same drawers and folders again and again throughout the day, even if I just looked a few minutes prior. It just feels better to know, you see? \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too terribly stubborn of me. I really am trying to be sensible about it. Does that sound like a reasonable way to feel?\n\nDoctor: Does this habit of checking your folders and drawers interfere with your ability to complete other tasks or activities throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I do try my very best to keep up with my schedule. I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting around neglecting my responsibilities! But... well, I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it has gotten just a little bit in the way recently. \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing to admit, Doctor, but I've actually had to... well, cancel a few of my mentoring sessions. And I skipped my book club meeting, too. You see, I start looking through my things, just to be sure they're safe, and before I know it, I've somehow lost the whole afternoon. The time just slips right by me. \n\nAnd then, of course, because I've been pulling out the folders and checking the boxes, the apartment gets into a bit of a disarray. I just feel far too embarrassed to have anyone over while it looks like that. I do hope you don't judge me too harshly for it. It’s just a silly little hurdle, I’m sure. Am I doing the right thing by telling you all this? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your folders and drawers is something you have to do, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, I certainly want to say it's just a choice. I like to think I'm still the one steering the ship, so to speak! I don't want to sound as though I've completely lost control of my own hands. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being perfectly honest with you—and I do want to be a good patient and give you the whole truth—that's actually the very reason I finally made this appointment. It really has started to feel like something I just have to do to settle my mind. \n\nI finally had to admit to myself that I spend far, far too much of my day doing it. I just find myself opening those exact same folders, the drawers, and my little labeled boxes again and again. Just to make absolutely sure my important papers and keepsakes are right where I left them... even when I know perfectly well I just checked them a few minutes before. \n\nI do hope that doesn't sound too dreadful of me to admit. I know it's a bit silly to keep looking at the same boxes. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm explaining it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"repeating thoughts\" makes it sound a bit dramatic, doesn't it? I certainly try my best to keep my mind on cheerful things, and I wouldn't want you to think my imagination is just running away from me! \n\nBut... well, I suppose since you ask, and I do want to give you the right information so you can help me... there is this one little worry that tends to get stuck. Even when I know better, and I'm trying to just sit quietly with a cup of tea or a book, a thought will just keep... needling me. I'll start worrying out of nowhere that I might have accidentally thrown away my late husband's old tax papers, or perhaps that I put a special family recipe in the wrong box. \n\nIt sounds so trivial when I say it out loud, but I just can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly, terribly careless. I just get this awful knot that I've made a dreadful mistake, and the thought just won't leave me be until I go look. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm being foolish for fretting over such small things. I really do try to push it out of my mind. Does that sound like the sort of thing you were asking about, Doctor? I want to make sure I'm understanding your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things in your folders and drawers need to be arranged in a specific way, or is it just about checking that they are there?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I don't think I'm quite as particular as all that. I'd hate for you to think I'm one of those terribly rigid people who needs every little paper clip facing the same direction! I've always thought of myself as rather easygoing, really. \n\nIt's... well, it's really just about making sure things haven't gone missing or been misplaced. I just need to see that my husband's papers are safe in their folder, and the recipes are in their box. Once I know they're there, I'm quite satisfied. I don't need them alphabetized or color-coded or anything so severe as that. \n\nI just want to be sure I haven't been careless and lost anything precious. That's quite normal for someone my age, isn't it? Just wanting to keep one's important things safe? I hope that's a helpful answer, Doctor. I want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific rituals or actions when you're checking your folders and drawers?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I certainly hope not! \"Rituals\" sounds so... well, it sounds a bit strange, doesn't it? Like I'm casting a spell or doing some sort of peculiar dance in my study! I would be quite embarrassed if anyone thought I was doing something like that. \n\nI just... well, I just open the drawer, take a look to make sure the folder is there, and close it again. I don't tap the desk three times or say a little rhyme or anything unusual. I just look with my own two eyes to ease my mind. \n\nI try very hard to be sensible about it, even if I do it more often than I should. It's really just a simple look-see. Does that make sense, Doctor? I do hope I'm answering your questions properly... I just want to make sure you know I'm not doing anything terribly odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your folders and drawers?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I don't think the house will catch fire or... or that anyone will fall ill just because I didn't check a drawer. That would be quite a silly thing to believe, wouldn't it? I certainly don't have an imagination like that. \n\nIt's really just... well, it's just my own peace of mind, I suppose. I don't think a catastrophe will strike, I just... I just wouldn't be able to settle until I knew for certain I hadn't been careless. It's more of an internal bother, really, just that nagging feeling of having made a mistake. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too foolish. I know I shouldn't let such small things bother me, and I do try my best to be rational about it. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were wondering about, Doctor? I want to be sure I'm giving you the right sort of answers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is in its right place?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely about the mistakes, Doctor. I really don't mind if things are a bit untidy—I'm not one of those terribly fussy people, I promise you. \n\nIt's just... well, even when I know better, even when I'm sitting right there in my chair, this... this thought will just keep needling me. A thought that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or perhaps put a precious family recipe in the wrong box by mistake. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud, because I know I haven't done it. But I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. It's just a fear of being irresponsible, really. I do hope that makes sense? I'm trying very hard to explain it exactly as it happens, so I'm not wasting your time. Is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers would decrease if you knew someone else had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... I'm really not sure, Doctor. I've never actually asked anyone else to check them for me. \n\nI suppose I just wouldn't want to be a nuisance to anyone, asking them to look through my old papers and things. It seems like such a silly thing to bother another person with, you know? So I just haven't ever tried it. \n\nIf I really think about it... well, maybe it would help a little if someone I trusted told me everything was perfectly fine. But honestly, I just don't know. Since it's my own worry about having made a mistake, I almost wonder if I would still feel that little urge to just look with my own eyes, just to be absolutely certain I hadn't done something careless. \n\nI'm so sorry, I wish I had a better answer for you! I just haven't ever had anyone else do it, so I can't say for sure how I'd feel. I do hope that's alright? I'm trying my best to give you the right information, I just wouldn't want to guess and tell you the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things in your folders and drawers need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. Like I might have mentioned just a moment ago, I'm really not a fussy person when it comes to things being perfectly neat. The papers can be... well, they can be a bit jumbled up, honestly. \n\nAs long as I know I haven't thrown something important away by mistake, I'm quite happy to just close the drawer, no matter how they look inside. I don't have any need to line things up or put them in alphabetical order or anything like that. \n\nI hope I'm not repeating myself too much, Doctor. I know some people prefer things just so, but that's never really been my trouble. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the clearest picture I can, so I'm not making your job any harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your folders and drawers if someone you trusted was responsible for organizing and keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, that's an interesting thought. I suppose... if my daughter or someone else I completely trust was entirely in charge of it... \n\nYou know, I'm really not certain. I haven't ever been in that situation since my husband passed. I try very hard to manage my own affairs so I don't become a burden on anyone. It's so important to me not to be a bother, you see? \n\nBut if they were completely responsible... well, I'd like to think I could just let it go. It would be lovely to just trust them and not worry. But... I wonder if that little nagging feeling might still creep in, just whispering that maybe *they* made a mistake, and I ought to just double-check, just to be absolutely safe. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm giving you such wishy-washy answers today! I just don't want to tell you the wrong thing when I haven't actually experienced it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm still being helpful to you, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is driven more by a fear of something going wrong if you don't, or by a need to feel in control of your belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... that’s quite a deep question, Doctor. I... I really don't know if I can say for sure. \n\nI certainly don't think of it as needing to be in control. That sounds a bit... well, a bit bossy or demanding, doesn't it? And I really try so hard not to be that sort of person. I'd hate for you to think I was trying to control everything or be difficult. \n\nI suppose... if I had to guess, it's more just that silly little fear of having made a mistake, like accidentally throwing away a bill or a special letter without realizing it. It's just a worry that something might go wrong because of my own carelessness. But truthfully, I haven't really thought about it in those exact terms before, so it's hard to say definitively. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm not giving you a very good answer again! Am I doing alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, I'd hate to be wasting your time with all my \"I don't knows.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better if you just accepted that sometimes things might not be perfectly in order, or does that thought not comfort you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose accepting that things aren't perfectly in order is... it's a nice thought, isn't it? \n\nBut like I was saying earlier, it's really not the order of things that bothers me. A messy drawer is perfectly fine by me! I really don't mind a little clutter at all. \n\nIt's more just... well, if I tried to tell myself not to worry about whether I threw away that important bank statement or a letter from my grandson... I don't think that would comfort me very much. I think I would just sit there feeling dreadful, worrying that I'd made a terrible mistake and that I was going to cause trouble for someone else because of my own silliness. \n\nSo, I suppose the thought doesn't really help, because it's not the neatness I'm fretting over. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm understanding your question correctly. I really am trying my best to give you the right information!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers has to be done in a specific way or sequence for you to feel satisfied?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, there isn't a special sequence or... or a routine to it. I don't have to check the top drawer first and then the bottom one, or anything rigid like that. I know some people have to do things in a very particular order, but I'm really not like that at all. \n\nIt's really just... well, it's just the sheer amount of time I spend doing it that's the trouble. That's actually why I came to see you, Doctor. I just... I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, the drawers, the little labeled boxes I have... just over and over again. \n\nI'll check to make sure the important papers and my little keepsakes are exactly where I left them, and then... well, I might just check them again a few minutes later. Even when I know I just looked! It's so silly, isn't it? I feel terribly foolish even admitting it out loud to you. I just can't seem to stop myself from going back to look again. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, Doctor. I know it's not very sensible of me. Am I explaining it alright? I want to make sure you have a clear picture of what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers gives you a sense of symmetry or balance in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, symmetry? Goodness, no... I don't think so. I hope it's alright to say that, but I really don't feel any sort of... balance or harmony when I do it. \n\nIf anything, it makes me feel a bit flustered and silly, to be perfectly honest with you. I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm one of those very fussy people who needs everything lined up just perfectly on a desk or anything like that. I'm really quite easygoing about most things, I promise! \n\nIt's really just that nagging little worry about losing an important paper, like I mentioned before, rather than trying to make things symmetrical. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I keep shooting down your questions! I do hope I'm still being helpful, Doctor. Is that alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is related to trying to be perfect or making sure everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I don't think I've ever been much of a perfectionist, to tell you the truth. I hope that's an alright answer! \n\nI really wouldn't want you to think I'm a terribly fussy or demanding sort of person. I'm actually quite happy to let things be a little untidy. If a stack of mail is messy or a drawer isn't perfectly organized, it truly doesn't bother me in the slightest. \n\nIt's really just... well, just that silly fear I mentioned earlier, about accidentally throwing away something important and causing a fuss for someone else. It's never about making things look exactly right or being perfect. \n\nI feel just awful saying no to so many of your questions, Doctor! Am I still giving you the information you need? I really am trying my very best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is more about avoiding a feeling of responsibility for a potential mistake or about being in charge of your belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh dear... I'm really not quite sure how to answer that one. I hope you don't mind, but... I don't think either of those quite fits for me. \n\nI certainly don't feel like I need to be in charge or... or assert control over my belongings, if that makes sense. I'm really not a controlling sort of person. And I wouldn't say it's about avoiding responsibility either. I mean, I'd feel terrible if I made a mistake, naturally, but... oh, I just don't know how to explain it properly. It's really just that silly little worry I mentioned before, rather than any grand thoughts about responsibility. \n\nI feel so dreadful that I can't give you a better answer! Am I being terribly unhelpful today? I really am trying to think about it the way you're asking, I just... I'm just not entirely sure. I hope it's alright that I don't have a clear yes or no for that one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers would decrease if you knew someone else had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... I'm really not sure, Doctor. I've never actually asked anyone else to check them for me. \n\nI suppose I just wouldn't want to be a nuisance to anyone, asking them to look through my old papers and things. It seems like such a silly thing to bother another person with, you know? So I just haven't ever tried it. \n\nIf I really think about it... well, maybe it would help a little if someone I trusted told me everything was perfectly fine. But honestly, I just don't know. Since it's my own worry about having made a mistake, I almost wonder if I would still feel that little urge to just look with my own eyes, just to be absolutely certain I hadn't done something careless. \n\nI'm so sorry, I wish I had a better answer for you! I just haven't ever had anyone else do it, so I can't say for sure how I'd feel. I do hope that's alright? I'm trying my best to give you the right information, I just wouldn't want to guess and tell you the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things in your folders and drawers need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. Like I might have mentioned just a moment ago, I'm really not a fussy person when it comes to things being perfectly neat. The papers can be... well, they can be a bit jumbled up, honestly. \n\nAs long as I know I haven't thrown something important away by mistake, I'm quite happy to just close the drawer, no matter how they look inside. I don't have any need to line things up or put them in alphabetical order or anything like that. \n\nI hope I'm not repeating myself too much, Doctor. I know some people prefer things just so, but that's never really been my trouble. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the clearest picture I can, so I'm not making your job any harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your folders and drawers if someone you trusted was responsible for organizing and keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, that's an interesting thought. I suppose... if my daughter or someone else I completely trust was entirely in charge of it... \n\nYou know, I'm really not certain. I haven't ever been in that situation since my husband passed. I try very hard to manage my own affairs so I don't become a burden on anyone. It's so important to me not to be a bother, you see? \n\nBut if they were completely responsible... well, I'd like to think I could just let it go. It would be lovely to just trust them and not worry. But... I wonder if that little nagging feeling might still creep in, just whispering that maybe *they* made a mistake, and I ought to just double-check, just to be absolutely safe. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm giving you such wishy-washy answers today! I just don't want to tell you the wrong thing when I haven't actually experienced it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm still being helpful to you, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is driven more by a fear of something going wrong if you don't, or by a need to feel in control of your belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... that’s quite a deep question, Doctor. I... I really don't know if I can say for sure. \n\nI certainly don't think of it as needing to be in control. That sounds a bit... well, a bit bossy or demanding, doesn't it? And I really try so hard not to be that sort of person. I'd hate for you to think I was trying to control everything or be difficult. \n\nI suppose... if I had to guess, it's more just that silly little fear of having made a mistake, like accidentally throwing away a bill or a special letter without realizing it. It's just a worry that something might go wrong because of my own carelessness. But truthfully, I haven't really thought about it in those exact terms before, so it's hard to say definitively. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm not giving you a very good answer again! Am I doing alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, I'd hate to be wasting your time with all my \"I don't knows.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better if you just accepted that sometimes things might not be perfectly in order, or does that thought not comfort you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose accepting that things aren't perfectly in order is... it's a nice thought, isn't it? \n\nBut like I was saying earlier, it's really not the order of things that bothers me. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm understanding your question correctly. I just need to see that my husband's papers are safe in their folder, and the recipes are in their box. Once I know they're there, I'm quite satisfied. That's quite normal for someone my age, isn't it? Just wanting to keep one's important things safe? I hope that's a helpful answer, Doctor. I want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific rituals or actions when you're checking your folders and drawers?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I certainly hope not! \"Rituals\" sounds so... well, it sounds a bit strange, doesn't it? Like I'm casting a spell or doing some sort of peculiar dance in my study! I would be quite embarrassed if anyone thought I was doing something like that. \n\nI just... well, I just open the drawer, take a look to make sure the folder is there, and close it again. I don't tap the desk three times or say a little rhyme or anything unusual. I just look with my own two eyes to ease my mind. \n\nI try very hard to be sensible about it, even if I do it more often than I should. It's really just a simple look-see. Does that make sense, Doctor? I do hope I'm answering your questions properly... I just want to make sure you know I'm not doing anything terribly odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't check your folders and drawers?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I don't think the house will catch fire or... or that anyone will fall ill just because I didn't check a drawer. That would be quite a silly thing to believe, wouldn't it? I certainly don't have an imagination like that. \n\nIt's really just... well, it's just my own peace of mind, I suppose. I don't think a catastrophe will strike, I just... I just wouldn't be able to settle until I knew for certain I hadn't been careless. It's more of an internal bother, really, just that nagging feeling of having made a mistake. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too foolish. I know I shouldn't let such small things bother me, and I do try my best to be rational about it. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were wondering about, Doctor? I want to be sure I'm giving you the right sort of answers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is in its right place?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely about the mistakes, Doctor. I really don't mind if things are a bit untidy—I'm not one of those terribly fussy people, I promise you. \n\nIt's just... well, even when I know better, even when I'm sitting right there in my chair, this... this thought will just keep needling me. A thought that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or perhaps put a precious family recipe in the wrong box by mistake. \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud, because I know I haven't done it. But I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. It's just a fear of being irresponsible, really. I do hope that makes sense? I'm trying very hard to explain it exactly as it happens, so I'm not wasting your time. Is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers would decrease if you knew someone else had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... I'm really not sure, Doctor. I've never actually asked anyone else to check them for me. \n\nI suppose I just wouldn't want to be a nuisance to anyone, asking them to look through my old papers and things. It seems like such a silly thing to bother another person with, you know? So I just haven't ever tried it. \n\nIf I really think about it... well, maybe it would help a little if someone I trusted told me everything was perfectly fine. But honestly, I just don't know. Since it's my own worry about having made a mistake, I almost wonder if I would still feel that little urge to just look with my own eyes, just to be absolutely certain I hadn't done something careless. \n\nI'm so sorry, I wish I had a better answer for you! I just haven't ever had anyone else do it, so I can't say for sure how I'd feel. I do hope that's alright? I'm trying my best to give you the right information, I just wouldn't want to guess and tell you the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things in your folders and drawers need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. Like I might have mentioned just a moment ago, I'm really not a fussy person when it comes to things being perfectly neat. The papers can be... well, they can be a bit jumbled up, honestly. \n\nAs long as I know I haven't thrown something important away by mistake, I'm quite happy to just close the drawer, no matter how they look inside. I don't have any need to line things up or put them in alphabetical order or anything like that. \n\nI hope I'm not repeating myself too much, Doctor. I know some people prefer things just so, but that's never really been my trouble. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the clearest picture I can, so I'm not making your job any harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your folders and drawers if someone you trusted was responsible for organizing and keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, that's an interesting thought. I suppose... if my daughter or someone else I completely trust was entirely in charge of it... \n\nYou know, I'm really not certain. I haven't ever been in that situation since my husband passed. I try very hard to manage my own affairs so I don't become a burden on anyone. It's so important to me not to be a bother, you see? \n\nBut if they were completely responsible... well, I'd like to think I could just let it go. It would be lovely to just trust them and not worry. But... I wonder if that little nagging feeling might still creep in, just whispering that maybe *they* made a mistake, and I ought to just double-check, just to be absolutely safe. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm giving you such wishy-washy answers today! I just don't want to tell you the wrong thing when I haven't actually experienced it. Does that make sense? I hope I'm still being helpful to you, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is driven more by a fear of something going wrong if you don't, or by a need to feel in control of your belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... that’s quite a deep question, Doctor. I... I really don't know if I can say for sure. \n\nI certainly don't think of it as needing to be in control. That sounds a bit... well, a bit bossy or demanding, doesn't it? And I really try so hard not to be that sort of person. I'd hate for you to think I was trying to control everything or be difficult. \n\nI suppose... if I had to guess, it's more just that silly little fear of having made a mistake, like accidentally throwing away a bill or a special letter without realizing it. It's just a worry that something might go wrong because of my own carelessness. But truthfully, I haven't really thought about it in those exact terms before, so it's hard to say definitively. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm not giving you a very good answer again! Am I doing alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to know, I'd hate to be wasting your time with all my \"I don't knows.\"\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better if you just accepted that sometimes things might not be perfectly in order, or does that thought not comfort you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose accepting that things aren't perfectly in order is... it's a nice thought, isn't it? \n\nBut like I was saying earlier, it's really not the order of things that bothers me. A messy drawer is perfectly fine by me! I really don't mind a little clutter at all. \n\nIt's more just... well, if I tried to tell myself not to worry about whether I threw away that important bank statement or a letter from my grandson... I don't think that would comfort me very much. I think I would just sit there feeling dreadful, worrying that I'd made a terrible mistake and that I was going to cause trouble for someone else because of my own silliness. \n\nSo, I suppose the thought doesn't really help, because it's not the neatness I'm fretting over. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm understanding your question correctly. I really am trying my best to give you the right information!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers has to be done in a specific way or sequence for you to feel satisfied?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, there isn't a special sequence or... or a routine to it. I don't have to check the top drawer first and then the bottom one, or anything rigid like that. I know some people have to do things in a very particular order, but I'm really not like that at all. \n\nIt's really just... well, it's just the sheer amount of time I spend doing it that's the trouble. That's actually why I came to see you, Doctor. I just... I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, the drawers, the little labeled boxes I have... just over and over again. \n\nI'll check to make sure the important papers and my little keepsakes are exactly where I left them, and then... well, I might just check them again a few minutes later. Even when I know I just looked! It's so silly, isn't it? I feel terribly foolish even admitting it out loud to you. I just can't seem to stop myself from going back to look again. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, Doctor. I know it's not very sensible of me. Am I explaining it alright? I want to make sure you have a clear picture of what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers gives you a sense of symmetry or balance in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, symmetry? Goodness, no... I don't think so. I hope it's alright to say that, but I really don't feel any sort of... balance or harmony when I do it. \n\nIf anything, it makes me feel a bit flustered and silly, to be perfectly honest with you. I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm one of those very fussy people who needs everything lined up just perfectly on a desk or anything like that. I'm really quite easygoing about most things, I promise! \n\nIt's really just that nagging little worry about losing an important paper, like I mentioned before, rather than trying to make things symmetrical. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I keep shooting down your questions! I do hope I'm still being helpful, Doctor. Is that alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is related to trying to be perfect or making sure everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I don't think I've ever been much of a perfectionist, to tell you the truth. I hope that's an alright answer! \n\nI really wouldn't want you to think I'm a terribly fussy or demanding sort of person. I'm actually quite happy to let things be a little untidy. It's never about making things look exactly right or being perfect. \n\nI feel just awful saying no to so many of your questions, Doctor! Am I still giving you the information you need? A messy drawer is perfectly fine by me! I don't think that would comfort me very much. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm understanding your question correctly. I really am trying my best to give you the right information!\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers has to be done in a specific way or sequence for you to feel satisfied?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, there isn't a special sequence or... or a routine to it. I don't have to check the top drawer first and then the bottom one, or anything rigid like that. I know some people have to do things in a very particular order, but I'm really not like that at all. \n\nIt's really just... well, it's just the sheer amount of time I spend doing it that's the trouble. That's actually why I came to see you, Doctor. I just... I spend far too much of my day opening those same folders, the drawers, the little labeled boxes I have... just over and over again. \n\nI'll check to make sure the important papers and my little keepsakes are exactly where I left them, and then... well, I might just check them again a few minutes later. Even when I know I just looked! It's so silly, isn't it? I feel terribly foolish even admitting it out loud to you. I just can't seem to stop myself from going back to look again. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, Doctor. I know it's not very sensible of me. Am I explaining it alright? I want to make sure you have a clear picture of what's going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking your folders and drawers gives you a sense of symmetry or balance in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, symmetry? Goodness, no... I don't think so. I hope it's alright to say that, but I really don't feel any sort of... balance or harmony when I do it. \n\nIf anything, it makes me feel a bit flustered and silly, to be perfectly honest with you. I certainly wouldn't want you to think I'm one of those very fussy people who needs everything lined up just perfectly on a desk or anything like that. I'm really quite easygoing about most things, I promise! \n\nIt's really just that nagging little worry about losing an important paper, like I mentioned before, rather than trying to make things symmetrical. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I keep shooting down your questions! I do hope I'm still being helpful, Doctor. Is that alright? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is related to trying to be perfect or making sure everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... I don't think I've ever been much of a perfectionist, to tell you the truth. I hope that's an alright answer! \n\nI really wouldn't want you to think I'm a terribly fussy or demanding sort of person. I'm actually quite happy to let things be a little untidy. If a stack of mail is messy or a drawer isn't perfectly organized, it truly doesn't bother me in the slightest. \n\nIt's really just... well, just that silly fear I mentioned earlier, about accidentally throwing away something important and causing a fuss for someone else. It's never about making things look exactly right or being perfect. \n\nI feel just awful saying no to so many of your questions, Doctor! Am I still giving you the information you need? I really am trying my very best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your folders and drawers is more about avoiding a feeling of responsibility for a potential mistake or about being in charge of your belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh dear... I'm really not quite sure how to answer that one. I hope you don't mind, but... I don't think either of those quite fits for me. \n\nI certainly don't feel like I need to be in charge or... or assert control over my belongings, if that makes sense. I'm really not a controlling sort of person. And I wouldn't say it's about avoiding responsibility either. I mean, I'd feel terrible if I made a mistake, naturally, but... oh, I just don't know how to explain it properly. It's really just that silly little worry I mentioned before, rather than any grand thoughts about responsibility. \n\nI feel so dreadful that I can't give you a better answer! Am I being terribly unhelpful today? I really am trying to think about it the way you're asking, I just... I'm just not entirely sure.
